is the (Hermitian) adjoint.

These 2-
\dagger
-unitarity properties will come into play once again in §
6.2
.

In the following section, we shall introduce a quantum deformation of the 2-graph operators from
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
to
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
, which promotes the antipode
S_{h}
in (
4.5
) to be generally non-involutive.

4.2
Quantum deformation on the lattice

Recall in the case of the coordinate ring
C(G)
for an ordinary Lie group
G
, a quantum deformation
\star
of its commutative product can be introduced from the data of a classical
r
-matrix on
\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Lie}G
, such that the
\star
-commutator
[-,-]_{\star}
is controlled to first order in
\hbar
by (
4.3
)
[
180
,
129
,
159
]
.

We are now tasked with two goals:

1.

quantize
the classical 2-
r
-matrix
r
on
\mathfrak{G}
to a quantum 2-
R
-matrix
R
, which act as quantum deformations of the structure (
4.2
),

R\sim 1+i\hbar r+o((i\hbar)^{2})

(see also
Remark
4.3
later).

2.

categorify
the deformed
\star
-product to the entire measureable category
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
.

The first point, for
C(\mathbb{G})
, was studied in
[
44
]
, which led to the motivating example for the notion of a 2-
R
-matrix there. Let us briefly recall this result.

4.2.1
Hopf 2-algebras and Baez-Crans 2-vector spaces

A
Baez-Crans 2-vector space

V\in\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}=\operatorname{Cat}_{\mathsf{Vect}}
is a category internal to
\mathsf{Vect}

[
12
,
107
]
. The central motivation for considering this setting is that Baez-Crans 2-vector spaces are the backdrop for the sort of
L_{\infty}
-(bi)algebras
[
47
,
24
,
50
]
and Hopf
A_{\infty}
-algebras
[
171
,
45
]
that arise in principal higher-bundles
[
23
,
158
,
140
,
179
,
109
]
and higher-gauge theory
[
21
,
161
,
162
,
42
]
.

Indeed, a Lie 2-algebra is nothing but a Lie algebra object in
\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}

[
12
]
, and this inspires the following notion introduced in
[
44
]
.

Definition 4.5
.

A
Hopf 2-algebra

A
is a Hopf algebra object in
\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}
.

A crucial characterization of Baez-Crans 2-vector spaces was given in
[
12
]
.

Proposition 4.6
.

There is an equivalence
\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}\simeq\mathsf{2Ch}(\mathsf{Vect})
with the 2-truncated 2-category of 2-term chain complexes.

One side of the equivalence, which associates a Baez-Crans 2-vector space
V=V_{1}\overset{s}{\underset{t}{\rightrightarrows}}V_{0}
with a 2-term chain complex
V_{-1}\xrightarrow{\mu_{1}}V_{0}
, is given by

V_{-1}=\operatorname{ker}s\subset V_{1},\qquad\mu_{1}(y)=x^{\prime}-x,

where
y:x\rightarrow x^{\prime}\in V_{1}
is a morphism.

Due to
Proposition
4.6
, the coproduct
\Delta=(\Delta_{0},\Delta_{1}):A[1]\rightarrow A^{\otimes 2}
on a Hopf 2-algebra
A=A_{-1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}}A_{0}
is a
differential graded
map, satisfying

(\mathsf{t}\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{t})\circ\Delta_{-1}=\Delta_{0}\circ\mathsf{t},\qquad(\mathsf{t}\otimes 1-1\otimes\mathsf{t})\circ\Delta_{0}=0.

(4.6)

The structure of the classical 2-
r
-matrix on
\mathfrak{G}

[
24
,
45
]
then suggests the following quasitriangularity structure.
7
7

7

In the ordinary quantum groups case, there is strictly speaking an obstruction to deformation quantization
[
66
,
159
]
. These are known to live as a certain degree-3 cohomology class of the underlying *-algebra, but such obstructions vanish for solution of the classical Yang-Baxter equation
[
118
]
. Similarly here: since the Lie 2-algebra cocycle
\delta
under consideration is determined by a solution to the 2-graded classical Yang-Baxter equations
[
24
]
, we expect such obstructions to vanish.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
A=A_{-1}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}}A_{0}
denote a Hopf 2-algebra. A
2-
R
-matrix

R
for
A
is an element
R\in A_{0}\otimes A_{-1}\oplus A_{-1}\otimes A_{0}
which satisfies the following graded conditions:

•

the
intertwining relation

\Delta^{\text{op}}(\mathrm{z})R=R\Delta(\mathrm{z}),\qquad\forall\penalty\ \mathrm{z}\in A,

•

the
equivariance condition

(\mathsf{t}\otimes 1-1\otimes\mathsf{t})R=0,

(4.7)

and

•

quasitriangularity conditions

(\Delta\otimes 1)R=R_{13}R_{12},\qquad(1\otimes\Delta)R=R_{13}R_{23},

(4.8)

•

the
antipode conditions

((1\otimes S)R)_{12}\cdot R_{23}=R_{12}\cdot((S\otimes 1)R)_{23}=\eta_{1}\eta_{3},

where the legs labelled by "2" are contracted, and

•

the obvious counit conditions
(1\otimes\epsilon)R=(\epsilon\otimes 1)R=\eta
.

It is not hard to show that
\bar{R}=(\mathsf{t}\otimes 1)R=(1\otimes\mathsf{t})R
satisfies

\bar{\Delta}_{0}^{\text{op}}(x)\bar{R}=\bar{R}\bar{\Delta}_{0}(x),\qquad\forall x\in A_{0},

where
\bar{\Delta}_{0}=(\mathsf{t}\otimes 1)\circ\Delta_{0}=(1\otimes\mathsf{t})\circ\Delta_{0}
. In fact, at degree-0,
(A_{0},S_{0},\bar{\Delta}_{0},\bar{R})
is an ordinary Hopf algebra equipped with a
R
-matrix.

Clearly, by thinking of
A
as a category internal to
\mathsf{Vect}
, then a(n invertible) 2-
R
-matrix induces, through the conjugation action, a bimonoidal automorphism

\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{ad}_{R}:A\otimes A\rightarrow A\otimes A,\qquad\mathcal{R}\circ\Delta=\Delta^{\text{op}}.

This is the crucial insight that we shall leverage below.

Remark 4.3
.

The condition (
4.7
) above implies that a 2-graded
R
-matrix for
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G})
can be "boostrapped" from a
R
-matrix for
C(G)
at degree-0. Further, if
\mathbb{G}=\operatorname{Inn}G
were the inner automorphism 2-group of a compact simple Lie group
G
with
t=\operatorname{id}
, then 2-
R
-matrices for
\operatorname{Inn}G
has a direct bijective correspondence with quantum
R
-matrices for
C(G)
through (
4.7
). As is well-known, solutions of the quantum Yang-Baxter equations have been extensively studied since the 80’s
[
98
,
66
,
129
,
180
,
150
,
185
,
117
,
73
]
. This observation is useful for constructing explicit examples of 2-Chern-Simons TQFTs.

\Diamond

The main result in
[
44
]
verifies that a quasitriangular 2-
R
-matrix on
A
endows the 2-category of 2-representations of
A
a braided monoidal structure.
Example 2.12.1
there states that the
\mathbb{C}
-valued function algebra
A=C(\mathbb{G})=C(G)\xrightarrow{t^{*}}C(\mathsf{H})
is a commutative Hopf 2-algebra.

Moreover, Appendix B of
[
44
]
proves the following.

Proposition 4.8
.

There exists a non-commutative quasitriangular Hopf 2-algebra equipped with a 2-
R
-matrix
R
, denoted
(C_{q}(\mathbb{G});R)
, which at first order in
\hbar
reduces to the Poisson-Lie 2-group
(C(\mathbb{G}),\{-,-\})

[
49
]
. Further, the Poisson bracket
\{-,-\}
is canonically induced by a classical 2-
r
-matrix
r
on the associated Lie 2-algebra
\mathfrak{G}

[
24
]
.

The associativity of
\star
thus follows from the strict Jacobi identity satisfied by the combinatorial 2-Fock-Rosly brackets (
4.2
).

Remark 4.4
.

In the weakly-associative case, we must deal semiclassically with (at least) a quasi-Lie 2-bialgebra, namely a Lie 2-bialgebra with non-trivial cohomotopy map
[
50
]
. It is known
[
49
]
that such a structure integrates to a
quasi-Poisson-Lie 2-group
, which has equipped a multiplicative trivector field
\eta
witnessing the Jacobi identity for the graded Poisson brackets. From the above construction, it then stands to reason that
\eta
gives rise to an associator for the quantum deformed monoidal structure on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
(see §
4.2.2
later).

\Diamond

Motivation for categorification.

The reader may wonder why we have chosen to work in the higher-categorical context
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G})
, instead of just working with the simpler function Hopf 2-algebra
C(\mathbb{G})
. Aside from the categorical ladder philosophy
[
9
]
(fig.
1
), there are multi-fold reasons:

1.

Mathematically, categorification bypasses many issues suffered by the Baez-Crans 2-vector spaces
\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}
and the Morita context it defines (see §
A.2.1
),

2.

Physically, Hopf 2-algebras based on Baez-Crans 2-vector spaces is
not
sufficient to describe correlation functions of Wilson surfaces and 2-holonomies, even in the strict case (see §
A.2.2
),

3.

Practically, categorification is necessary in order to detect
k
-invariants and higher-codimensional defects arising from higher-gauge symmetry; for instance, the module associators
\alpha^{\Lambda}
(see
Remark
3.4
) would not be present otherwise.

4.2.2
Lifting the quantum product to measureable sheaves

To categorify the above structures, we need to invoke the main result of
[
38
]
:

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
X
denote a Riemannian manifold. A fixed
\star
-product on
C(X)
determines uniquely (up to isometry on
X
) a
\star
-product on the smooth sections
\Gamma(X,E)
of a vector bundle
E\rightarrow X
. The resulting sheaf of
\star
-deformed global sections, denoted
\Gamma(X,E)[[\hbar]]
, is a
C(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
-module
C^{*}
-algebras.

Since the
classical
2-graph operators
\phi\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
are modelled as modules over the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}=C(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
on
X=(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma},\mu_{\Gamma})
, then once we know the
\star
-deformation
C(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightsquigarrow C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
through (
4.2
) and
Proposition
4.8
, we can leverage
Theorem
4.9
to categorify the
\star
-product and the 2-
R
-matrix.
8
8

8

Technically, we would like to use a generalization of
Theorem
4.9
to quasicoherent sheaves. It was noted in
[
38
]
that their results hold for sheaves given by generic projective modules.

Denote by
\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})[[\hbar]]
the sheaf of
formal power series
in the global sections over
X=(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma},\mu_{\Gamma})
. We now construct a categorical
deformed tensor product
from the underlying
\star
-product. For this, it would be useful to recall the following general fact about sheaves
[
77
]
.

Theorem 4.10
.

There is a canonical isomorphism
\Gamma(X,{\cal F}\otimes_{{\cal O}_{X}}{\cal F}^{\prime})\cong\Gamma(X,{\cal F})\otimes_{{\cal O}_{X}}\Gamma(X,{\cal F}^{\prime})
for any sheaves
{\cal F},{\cal F}^{\prime}
over
X
.

When applied to sheaves of global sections in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
, this means that there are canonical isomorphisms

\phi\otimes\phi^{\prime}=\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})\otimes\Gamma_{c}(H^{\prime X})\cong\Gamma_{c}((H\otimes H^{\prime})^{X})

(4.9)

of
C(X)
-modules for each
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
, where
X=(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma},\mu_{\Gamma})
.

Our goal is to promote the canonical isomorphism (
4.9
) to the quantum deformed case, with
q=e^{i\hbar}
, by using
Theorem
4.9
. This will require the assumption that there exists a "decategorification"
\lambda:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\mapsto C_{q}(X)=C(X)\otimes\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
sending measureable
C_{q}(X)
-module
\star
-algebras to
C_{q}(X)
. See
Definition
4.13
later for more details on this assumption.

Definition 4.11
.

The
deformed tensor product
is a monoidal structure
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
on the 2-graph operators
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
such that

1.

we have natural sheaf isomorphisms

\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})[[\hbar]]\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\Gamma_{c}(H^{\prime X})[[\hbar]]\cong\Gamma_{c}((H\otimes H^{\prime})^{X})[[\hbar]],

(4.10)

linear over
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
, for all
\Gamma_{c}(H^{X}),\Gamma_{c}(H^{\prime X})\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
and

2.

\lambda
is "(strictly) monoidal", in the sense that on the essential image of
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
, we have an isomrphism

\lambda(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\cong(\lambda-\star\lambda-)

(4.11)

of algebra in
\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}=\operatorname{Cat}_{\mathsf{Vect}}
.

At the same time, we will assume that
\lambda
fits in a commutative diagram for the
coproducts
, analogous to the above. We call
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
the
lift
of
\star
along the decategorification
\lambda
.

In the undeformed case, we of course recover the usual tensor product
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}=\otimes
and the commutative ring
C(X)
; see
Remark
4.5
. This will be important in §
4.3.2
later.

The first part of this definition allows us to define the
\star
-deformed product between global sections of
any
two measureable sheaves, and the second part states that
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
is determined up to isomorphism by this
\star
-product.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the vertical arrows are given by "evaluating" at
\hbar=0
;
(-)_{0}:\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})[[\hbar]]\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})
.

Proof.

Let
\phi=\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})[[\hbar]]
and
\phi^{\prime}=\Gamma_{c}(H^{\prime X})[[\hbar]]
be objects in
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
. We have the natural isomorphism

(\phi\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\phi^{\prime})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{deformedtensor}}}{{\cong}}\Gamma_{c}((H\otimes H^{\prime})^{X})\cong\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})\otimes\Gamma_{c}(H^{\prime X})=(\phi)_{0}\otimes(\phi^{\prime})_{0}

(4.12)

provided by the universal property (
4.9
) of the tensor product.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Let us describe a simpler incarnation of
\lambda
in a more concrete way. Consider the Kapranov-Veovodsky model of "2-vector spaces"
\mathsf{2Vect}=\mathsf{2Vect}^{KV}

[
102
]
given by fully-fualizable
finite
semisimple linear categories
C\in\mathsf{2Vect}.
Denote by
\mathsf{sepMor}
the Morita bicategory of separable
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, it is well-known that there is an equivalence
\mathsf{sepMor}\simeq\mathsf{2Vect}
given by sending
A\mapsto\operatorname{Mod}(A)
to its category of modules
[
71
]
. Define the following 2-functor (the subscript "gl" stands for "global")

\lambda_{\text{gl}}:\mathsf{2Vect}\rightarrow\mathsf{sepAlg}_{\mathbb{C}},\qquad C\simeq\operatorname{Mod}(A)\mapsto A

which is left-adjoint to the equivalence
\mathsf{sepMor}\simeq\mathsf{2Vect}
. The analogue of the diagram (
4.11
) in this context is saying that
\lambda_{\text{gl}}
preserves the monoidal structure of a
particular
algebra object
C\in\mathsf{2Vect}
, such that

\lambda_{\text{gl}}(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)=\lambda_{\text{gl}}(-)\star\lambda_{\text{gl}}(-),\qquad\begin{cases}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}:C\boxtimes C\rightarrow C\\
\star:A\otimes A\rightarrow A\end{cases}.

In other words, under the the Deligne tensor product, the algebra structure
\star:A\otimes A\to A
lifts to its modules

\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}:\operatorname{Mod}(A)\boxtimes\operatorname{Mod}(A)\simeq\operatorname{Mod}(A\otimes A)\to\operatorname{Mod}(A)\,.

For us, we require a "sheafy", local-systems version of
\lambda_{\text{gl}}
, which sends
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module algebras over
X
to the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
itself. The roles of
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
and its structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}=C_{q}(X)=C(X)\otimes\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]
, for
q=e^{i\hbar}
, correspond respectively to the particular algebra objects
C\in\mathsf{2Vect}
and
A\in\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

\Diamond

Definition 4.13
.

Hypothesis (H)
is the assumption that there exists a limit-preserving 2-functor
\mathsf{Meas}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vect}
(possibly infinite-dimensional vector spaces) such that

1.

it induces the decategorification
\lambda:\operatorname{Cat}_{\mathsf{Meas}}\rightarrow\operatorname{Cat}_{\mathsf{Vect}}=\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}
,

2.

for any 2-graph complex
\Gamma
, the induced map
\lambda:\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\mapsto C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
(i) satisfies the conditions in
Definition
4.11
, and (ii) preserves the coproduct
\Delta_{h}
and the antipode
S_{h}
.

Hypothesis (H) is required in order for us to endow a monoidal grading (cf.
[
164
]
) to a bimonoidal (co)category internal to
\mathsf{Meas}
given by a
Hopf 2-algebra

A\in\mathsf{2Vect}^{BC}
, while allowing us to keep additivity at all levels (morphisms, objects); see §
A.2.1
for more details.

4.2.3
Categorical
R
-matrix

Given the above setup, we shall leverage (
4.11
) to define a
categorical
R
-matrix
, also denoted by
R\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
, such that by
Definition
4.13

\lambda
sends it to the 2-
R
-matrix on
C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
arising from the 2-Chern-Simons action. We can then write, as a slight abuse of notation, the following

[\phi_{(e,f)},\phi_{(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})}]_{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}}=(-\otimes-)\Big(R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\Delta_{h}(\phi_{(e,f)\cup_{h}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})})-\Delta^{\text{op}}_{\mathrm{h}}(\phi_{(e,f)\cup_{h}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R^{T}\Big),

(4.13)

to denote the
Hilbert spaces
of sections obtained from localized 2-graph operators
\phi=\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})[[\hbar]]
.

We now consider some coherence conditions satisfied by the
R
-matrix
R
. First, we will impose the natural compatibility against the pullbacks
\hat{s}^{*},\hat{t}^{*}
of the source and target maps
\hat{s},\hat{t}:(\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}}\rightrightarrows G^{\Gamma^{1}}
on the 2-holonomies
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}
. By taking the following components

R=R|_{\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})^{\times 2}},\qquad R_{0}=R|_{\mathfrak{C}_{q}(G^{\Gamma^{1}})^{\times 2}}

of the higher
R
-matrix, this naturality condition is expressed as

\displaystyle(R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\circ(\hat{s}^{*}\times\hat{s}^{*})=(\hat{s}^{*}\times\hat{s}^{*})\circ(R_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-),

\displaystyle(R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\circ(\hat{t}^{*}\times\hat{t}^{*})=(\hat{t}^{*}\times\hat{t}^{*})\circ(R_{0}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-),

where we have abused notation and denote by the canonical monoidal structure on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
also by
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
.

Recall the strictly coassociative and cointerchanging 2-graph coproducts on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
introduced in
Definition
4.1
. Their quantum versions, denoted also by
\Delta_{h},\Delta_{v}
, must then satisfy the following
intertwining relations
against the higher
R
-matrix: there exist natural sheaf isomorphisms

\displaystyle(\sigma\circ\Delta_{h})(\phi)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R\cong R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\Delta_{h}(\phi),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathrm{v}}\circ(R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\cong((R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\times(R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-))\circ\Delta_{v}

where
\sigma:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
is a swap of tensor factors.

(4.87)

Here,
-^{\text{op}}
denotes the
opposite cocateory
— namely a swap of the tensor factors in the summands of the vertical coproduct, and we have used the shorthand (
4.1
) to write

(\Delta_{h}\otimes\Delta_{h})\Delta_{\mathrm{v}}(\phi)=\Delta_{h}(\phi_{(1)})\times_{v}\Delta_{h}(\phi_{(2)})=(\phi_{(1)(1)}\times_{h}\phi_{(1)(2)})\otimes_{\mathrm{v}}(\phi_{(2)(1)}\times_{h}\phi_{(2)(2)}),\qquad\text{etc.}

for the coproducts. These diagrams come with dual diagrams with the
\mathrm{h,v}
swapped.

The arrows labelled by "
R^{i;j}
" implements a conjugation by the
R
-matrix (
4.2.3
) on the
i,j
-th factor. The
\beta
’s denote the witness for the cointerchange law (
3.2
) on the 2-graph operators, which we recall can be trivialized by going on-shell of the 2-flatness condition. We will prove in
Lemma
4.18
that these quantum deformed coproducts (
4.2.3
) are compatible in a Hopf categorical sense with a deformed monoidal structure
\otimes_{q}=\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
.

Note the property of having
two
types of (co)products and one product is shared by
cotrialgebras

[
143
]
(mentioned also in fig.
1
). However, here we have much more structure: this is the subject of the following section §
4.3
.

4.3
Higher Hopf structures on the 2-graph operators

Given the above quantum deformed corpdoucts and
R
-matrices, we now investigate the structure of the 2-graph operators
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
. Since these were induced through dualization directly from the 2-groupoid structure of the 2-group
\mathbb{G}
or the geometry of the 2-graphs
\Gamma
.

4.3.1
As a Hopf internal category

We first fix the definitions, then we get to work.

Internal categories.

Definition 4.14
.

A
category
C
internal to
{\cal V}
is a strict category object in a bicategory
{\cal V}
with pushouts and pullbacks (such as
{\cal C}=\mathsf{Meas}
). It consists of the data:

•

a pair of objects
C_{1},C_{0}\in{\cal C}
,

•

a pair of
fibrant
1-morphisms
s,t:C_{1}\rightarrow C_{0}
in
{\cal V}
called the
source/target
, and their pullback
C_{1}\,{}_{t}\times_{s}C_{1}
,

•

a 1-morphism
\circ:C_{1}\,{}_{t}\times_{s}C_{1}\rightarrow C_{1}
in
{\cal V}
, called the
composition law
, and

•

a 1-morphism
\eta:C_{0}\rightarrow C_{1}
, called the
unit
, such that

1.

the composition law
\circ
is strictly associative: the 2-morphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}\times\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\times\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}

is invertible,

2.

\circ,1
satisfy strict unity: for each
f\in C_{1}
with
s(f)=x
and
t(f)=y
, we have invertible 2-morphisms
1_{y}\circ f\cong f\cong f\circ 1_{x}
,

3.

the invertible compositional unitors and associators satisfy

(a)

the exchange equation (which we call the
interchange law
),

(b)

the left- and right-pentagon equations, and

(c)

the left-, middle- and right-triangle equations,

on the pullbacks
C_{1}^{[n]}=C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}\times_{C_{0}}\dots\times_{C_{0}}C_{1}
.

A
cocategory
D
internal to
{\cal V}
is a strict category object in
{\cal V}^{\text{op}}
. It is equipped with
cofibrant
functors
u,v:D_{0}\rightarrow D_{1}
, a strict counit
\epsilon:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{0}
and a strictly coassociative cocomposition law
\Delta_{v}:D_{1}\rightarrow D_{1}\penalty\ {}_{v}\times_{u}D_{1}
along the pushout.

Keep in mind that internal categories do not have cocompositions, and cocategories do not have compositions.

A (strict) functor
F:C\rightarrow D
of categories internal to
{\cal V}
is of course a pair of 1-morphisms
F_{i}:C_{i}\rightarrow D_{i}
for
i=0,1
, equipped with invertible 2-morphisms

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\qquad F(\circ)\cong\circ(F\times F),\qquad F_{1}\eta_{C}\cong\eta_{D}F_{0}

which ensures that
F
commutes with the fibrant source/target maps and the composition.

Let
\operatorname{Cat}_{{\cal V}},\operatorname{Cocat}_{{\cal V}}
denote the categories/cocategories internal to
{\cal V}
, respectively, then the canonical equivalence
{\cal V}\simeq{\cal V}^{\text{op}}
induces

\operatorname{Cocat}_{\cal V}\simeq\operatorname{Cat}_{{\cal V}^{\text{op}}},

which will play a big role in this paper.

Hopf internal categories.

Now suppose
{\cal V}
is symmetric monoidal, with a monoidal unit object
I\in{\cal V}
. As an abuse of notation, we will also denote by
I
its discrete category
I\rightrightarrows I
internal to
{\cal V}
.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
({\cal V},\times,I)
be a (
\mathbb{C}
-linear) symmetric monoidal 2-category. A
(strict) Hopf monoidal category
{\cal H}
in
{\cal V}
is a Hopf algebra object in
\operatorname{Cat}_{{\cal V}}
. Namely, it is equipped with the following internal functors:

1.

the
product

\otimes:{\cal H}\times{\cal H}\rightarrow{\cal H}
(with a unit
\iota\in{\cal H}
),

2.

the strictly monoidal
coproduct

\Delta:{\cal H}\rightarrow{\cal H}\times{\cal H}
(with a counit
\epsilon:{\cal H}\rightarrow I
), and

3.

the strictly op-comonoidal op-monoidal
antipode

S:{\cal H}\rightarrow{\cal H}^{\text{m-op},\text{c-op}}
,

as well as internal natural transformations

1.

the
associators

a^{\otimes}:\otimes\circ(\otimes\times 1_{\cal H})\Rightarrow\otimes\circ(1_{\cal H}\times\otimes)
and
unitors

r^{\otimes}:(-\otimes\iota)\Rightarrow 1_{\cal H},\penalty\ \ell^{\otimes}:(\iota\otimes-)\rightarrow 1_{\cal H}
satisfying the strict pentagon and triangle axioms,

2.

the
coassociators

a^{\Delta}:(\Delta\times 1_{\cal H})\circ\Delta\Rightarrow(1_{\cal H}\times\Delta)\Rightarrow\Delta
and
counitors

r^{\Delta}:(\epsilon\times 1_{\cal H})\circ\Delta\Rightarrow 1_{\cal H},\penalty\ \ell^{\Delta}:(1_{\cal H}\times\epsilon)\circ\Delta\Rightarrow 1_{\cal H}
satisfying the strict copentagon and cotriangle) axioms,

3.

the invertible
bimonoidal natural transformations

\Delta\circ\otimes\cong(1_{\cal H}\times\sigma\times 1_{\cal H})\circ(\otimes\times\otimes)\circ\Delta

(4.88)

4.

the
antipode relations

\otimes\circ(S\times 1_{\cal H})\circ\Delta\cong\iota\otimes\epsilon\cong\otimes\circ(1_{\cal H}\times S)\circ\Delta,

(4.89)

such that these internal natural transformations are mutually coherent.

A
(strict) Hopf comonoidal cocategory in
{\cal V}
is a (strict) Hopf monoidal category in
{\cal V}^{\text{op}}
.

The main example we shall consider in this paper is the symmetric monoidal 2-category
{\cal V}=\mathsf{Meas}
of Crane-Yetter measureable categories
[
56
,
183
,
10
]
.

Recall that the bicategory
\mathsf{Meas}
of Crane-Yetter measureable categories
[
56
,
183
]
can be understood as
W^{*}
-modules over
L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, which is a commutative von Neumann algebra.

Definition 4.16
.

Denote by
\mathsf{Meas}_{q}
the bicategory of
W^{*}
-module categories over the non-commutative von Neumann algebra
\mathbb{C}[[\hbar]]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
— namely, the objects are categories of measuareable sheaves, whose global sections are also power series in
\hbar
.

Similar framework has been used in
[
113
]
, but for us the extent of non-commutativity of our von Neumann algebras is strictly controlled by the power series in
\hbar
. It can be thought of as the infinite-dimensional version of
\mathsf{2Vect}
(see
Remark
4.5
), or as a sheafy measureable version of the bicommutant categories of Henriques-Pennys
[
94
]
.

We now work to prove the main theorem by breaking it up into a few lemmas.

Lemma 4.17
.

\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
is an additive comonoidal cocategory internal to
\mathsf{Meas}_{q}
, with a coproduct and cocomposition.

Proof.

Additivity follows from additivity of the target
\mathsf{Hilb}
. Let
\hat{s},\hat{t}
denote the source and target maps in the 2-groupoid
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}=\operatorname{Fun}(\Gamma^{2},\mathbf{B}\mathbb{G})
as the confluence of those in
\mathbb{G}
and
\Gamma^{2}
. This confluence is well-defined on-shell of the fake-flatness condition

h_{\partial f}=\mathsf{t}(b_{f}),\qquad\forall\penalty\ (h_{e},b_{f})\in\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}.

(4.90)

Since
\hat{s},\hat{t}
are by hypothesis surjective submersive
[
126
,
49
]
, their pullbacks induce measureable functors
[
10
]
which are cofibrant

\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})\xleftarrow{\hat{s}^{*}}\mathfrak{C}_{q}(G^{\Gamma^{1}})\xrightarrow{\hat{t}^{*}}\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})

with a coidentity
\epsilon:\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})\to\mathfrak{C}_{q}(G^{\Gamma^{1}})
given by pulling back the unit section
\operatorname{id}:g\mapsto(g,1)
on
\mathbb{G}
. The coidentity represents the trivial face-localized state.

The cocomposition is given by the vertical coproduct
\Delta_{v}:\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})\times\mathfrak{C}_{q}((\mathsf{H}\rtimes G)^{\Gamma^{2}})
, which inserts a face-localized state in between two edge-localized states. The comonoidal coproduct is an additive measureable functor
\Delta_{h}:\tilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
given by the horizontal coproduct.

By
Proposition
4.2
,
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
is an additive comonoidal cocategory internal to
\mathsf{Meas}_{q}
.
∎

The next step is to prove that the quantum deformed product
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
satisfies (
4.88
). We will check this condition is first satisfied classically, then check that the combinatorial 2-Fock-Rosly Poisson brackets (
4.2
) are compatible.

Lemma 4.18
.

Provided the map
\lambda
preserves the coproducts, then (
4.88
) holds for
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
.

Proof.

We first establish (
4.88
) classically. On localized sections, properties of the tensor product
\otimes
and the geometry of the 2-holonomies then provide sheaf isomorphisms

\displaystyle(\Delta(\xi\otimes\xi^{\prime}))(h_{e},b_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum(\xi\otimes\xi^{\prime})_{(1)}(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})\times(\xi\otimes\xi^{\prime})_{(2)}(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum((\xi_{(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})})_{(1)}\otimes(\xi^{\prime}_{(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})})_{(1)})\times((\xi_{(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})})_{(2)}\otimes(\xi^{\prime}_{(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})})_{(2)})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum((\xi_{(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})})_{(1)}\times(\xi_{(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})})_{(2)})\otimes\sum((\xi^{\prime}_{(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})})_{(1)}\times(\xi^{\prime}_{(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})})_{(2)})

\displaystyle=(\Delta\xi)(h_{e},b_{f})\otimes(\Delta\xi^{\prime})(h_{e},b_{f})=(\Delta\xi\otimes\Delta\xi^{\prime})(h_{e},b_{f})

for any sections
\xi,\xi^{\prime}
of sheaves
\phi,\phi\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
localized on
(e,f)\in\Gamma^{2}
. Here
\Delta
can mean either the horizontal or vertical 2-graph coproduct.

Now semiclassically, the classical 2-Yang-Baxter equation (leading to the Lie 2-algebra cocycle condition for
\delta=dr\in Z^{2}(\mathfrak{G},\mathfrak{G}\wedge\mathfrak{G})

[
24
]
) directly implies the multiplicativity
[
49
]

\Delta_{h}(\{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\})=\{\Delta_{h}(\xi),\Delta_{h}(\xi^{\prime})\},\qquad\Delta_{v}(\{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\})=\{\Delta_{v}(\xi),\Delta_{v}(\xi^{\prime})\}

of the Poisson bracket (
4.2
) against the group/groupoid multiplication in
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}
, where we have implicitly applied the sheaf isomorphism (
4.10
). Quantizing lifts to the multiplicativity of the coproducts
\Delta_{\mathrm{h,v}}
against the
\star
-product on measureable sections,

\Delta_{h}(\xi\star\xi^{\prime})=\Delta_{h}(\xi)\star\Delta_{h}(\xi^{\prime}),\qquad\Delta_{v}(\xi\star\xi^{\prime})=\Delta_{v}(\xi)\star\Delta_{v}(\xi^{\prime}).

If Hypothesis (H) holds, namely that
\lambda
preserves the coproducts, then this multiplicativity lifts by
Definition
4.11
to
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
and (
4.88
) follows.

∎

Given the above result,
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
then gives rise to a
non-symmetric
cocategory.

Definition 4.19
.

Let
{\cal H}
denote a Hopf cocategory with the comonoidal coproduct functor
\Delta_{h}:{\cal H}\rightarrow{\cal H}\times{\cal H}
. A(n invertible)
cobraiding

(\mathcal{R},\mathsf{T})
on
{\cal H}
is an additive bimonoidal automorphism
\mathcal{R}:{\cal H}\times{\cal H}\rightarrow{\cal H}\times{\cal H}
equipped with a natural transformation

\mathsf{T}:\mathcal{R}\circ\Delta_{h}\Rightarrow\Delta_{h}^{\text{op}},

satisfying various Hopf coherence conditions against the higher morphisms in
Definition
4.15
.

We finally can state the following.

Theorem 4.20
.

\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
is a
cobraided
Hopf cocategory internal to
\mathsf{Meas}_{q}
.

Proof.

•

The antipode
: Given 2-
\dagger
-unitarity
Definition
4.1.2
, the antipode functors
S_{h,v}
promoted to the quantum 2-graph operators
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
in §
4.1.2
. The antipode axioms (
4.89
) then follow directly from the underlying geometry of
\Gamma^{2}
.

Together with (
4.2.3
), one can also verify that there exist (internal) natural transformations witnessing the following identity:

(S_{h}\times 1)R\penalty\ \hat{\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}}\penalty\ R\cong(1\times S_{h})R\cong 1_{\mathbb{C}}\otimes 1_{\mathbb{C}},

(4.91)

where
1_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
denotes the monoidal unit represented by the trivial measuereable field over
(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma},\mu_{\Gamma})
.

•

The cobraiding
: We define the cobraiding
(\mathcal{R},\mathsf{T})
on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})=\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
to be the following. Suppressing the strict associators, the automorphism
\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{ad}_{R}
is given by a
conjugation

\mathcal{R}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})=R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R^{-1},\qquad\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})

by the invertible (with respect to
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}^{\times 2}
on
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
) higher
R
-matrix
R
. Each component
\mathsf{T}_{\phi}
of the natural transformation are then given by (
4.2.3
).

By definition,
\mathcal{R}
is monoidal. We thus now need to prove the following:

1.

\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{ad}_{R}:\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
defines a functor between cocategories, and

2.

multiplication by
R
is comonoidal, ie. compatible with
\Delta_{h}
.

For the first point, we need
\mathcal{R}
to (i) commute with the cosource/cotarget maps on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
and (ii) is natural with respect to the cocomposition
\Delta_{v}
. (i) is guaranteed by (
4.2.3
), while (ii) is guaranteed by (
4.2.3
) and the diagram (
4.87
).

Now for the second point, we require higher quasitriangularity relations for
R
; namely that there exist isomorphisms in
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})^{\times 3}
,

Q_{\ell}:(1\times\Delta_{h})R\cong R_{13}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R_{23},\qquad Q_{r}:(\Delta_{h}\times 1)R\cong R_{13}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R_{12},

(4.92)

which are consistent with the
\Delta_{h}
-coassociator. These follow from (
4.86
) (see also
Lemma
4.22
later).

∎

By construction, the decategorification
\lambda
of
Definition
4.11
simply restricts to the structure sheaf
C_{q}(\mathbb{G})
and trivializes the natural transformation
\mathsf{T}
.

Definition 4.21
.

The
categorical quantum coordinate ring

\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
is the 2-graph operators
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
on the 2-graph
\Gamma
consisting of only a single face
f:e\rightarrow e
, a single edge loop
v\xrightarrow{e}v\in\Gamma^{1}
and a single vertex
v\in\Gamma^{0}
.

See also
Remark
3.3
.

Remark 4.6
.

Let us elaborate a bit more on the cobraiding. Suppose
\mathbb{G}
is a strict Lie 2-group such that we can suppress the co/associators. For each
\phi\in\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
, a cobraiding
(\mathcal{R},\mathsf{T})
as above is given by
\mathcal{R}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})=R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R^{-1}
, together with measureable natural isomorphisms

\mathsf{T}_{\phi}:R\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\Delta(\phi)\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R^{-1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Delta^{\text{op}}(\phi).

The components
\mathsf{T}_{R_{(1)}},\mathsf{T}_{R_{(2)}}
, together with the isomorphisms
Q_{\ell},Q_{r}
(
4.92
), give rise to invertible natural transformations

R_{23}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}(R_{13}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R_{12})\cong(R_{12}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R_{13})\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}R_{23}

which witness the Yang-Baxter equations (cf. (
4.95
)). Such Yang-Baxter intertwiners are known
[
114
]
to be very closely related to solutions of the
Zamolodchikov tetrahedron equations

[
184
,
102
]
.

\Diamond

We note that the generalization to the weakly-associative case can be carried out directly, by keeping track of the appearance of
\tau
and (its first descendant mentioned in §
3.1.2
) through natural ismorphisms. We expect to obtain a Hopf cocategory as well in this case, but with coassociative and cointerchange witnesses which need not only have identity components.

4.3.2
The semiclassical limit

The goal of this subsection is to show that the cobraiding on the 2-graph operators comes from a
quantum 2-
R
-matrix
on
C_{q}(X)
, as a
(quasi)triangular
Hopf 2-algebra as in §
4.2.1
and
[
44
]
. We will use this to determine the semiclassical limit.

We now work to recover
R
as a 2-
R
-matrix in the sense given in §
4.2.1
.

Lemma 4.22
.

Provided
\lambda
preserves the bimonoidal structure, the cobraiding on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
reduces to a 2-
R
-matrix on
C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we work directly with
\Gamma^{2}
given by the fundamental 2-graph. We prove that the component of
R
restricted to the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}}=C_{q}(\mathbb{G})
is a quantum 2-
R
-matrix
R
for the Hopf 2-algebra

A=A_{1}=C_{q}(\mathsf{H})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}^{*}}A_{0}=C_{q}(G),

where
\mathsf{t}^{*}
is the pullback of the Lie group crossed-module map
\mathsf{t}:\mathsf{H}\rightarrow G
.

From the quantum
R
-matrix
\lambda(R)
, we define
\Delta^{\prime}=\lambda(\Delta)
and

R^{\prime}=\lambda(R)|_{A_{1}\otimes A_{0}}\oplus\lambda(R)|_{A_{0}\otimes A_{1}}\equiv R^{l}\oplus R^{r},

(4.93)

such that
R^{\prime}\in(C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}))_{1}^{\otimes 2}
is an element of total degree-1.

Clearly (
4.2.3
) implies the intertwining property of
R
. Moreover, given the characterization
Proposition
4.6
, (
4.2.3
) reduces to (
4.7
). It now remains to recover the quasitriangularity conditions. To do so, we apply
\lambda
to the diagrams (
4.87
), (
4.86
).

Consider the arrow
R^{23;14}_{\mathrm{h}}
in (
4.86
). It involves the quantity
(\Delta_{h}\otimes\Delta_{h})R
, while the expressions we want are

(1\otimes\Delta_{h})R,\qquad(\Delta_{h}\otimes 1)R.

These can be computed from various contractions in (
4.86
). Indeed, by contracting the tensor legs labelled by
2,3
with
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
and putting
\phi_{(2)}\times\phi_{3}=\phi
, we obtain the commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi_{(1)}\times_{h}(\phi\times_{h}\phi_{4})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\phi_{(1)}\times_{h}\phi_{4})\times_{h}\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\phi\times_{h}(\phi_{(1)}\times_{h}\phi_{4})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{13}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta_{h}\otimes 1)R}

which gives, under
\lambda
(here the superscripts on
R
indicate which tensor factor the
R
-matrices act on, not the subscripts of the
\phi
’s),

(\Delta^{\prime}\otimes 1)R^{\prime}=R^{\prime 13}\otimes R^{\prime 12}.

Similarly we have

(1\otimes\Delta^{\prime})R^{\prime}=R^{\prime 13}\otimes R^{\prime 23}.

(4.94)

These are nothing but the quasitriangularity conditions.
∎

The conditions (
4.3.2
), (
4.94
) and (
4.2.3
) were shown in Proposition 3.13 of
[
44
]
to be equivalent to the "2-Yang-Baxter equations"

R^{\prime 23}(R^{\prime 13}R^{\prime 12})=(R^{\prime 12}R^{\prime 13})R^{\prime 23}

(4.95)

on
C_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
.

We can now prove that
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
admits the Lie 2-bialgebra
(\mathfrak{G};\delta)
underlying the 2-Chern-Simons action
[
43
]
as its semiclassical limit, with
\mathfrak{G}=\operatorname{Lie}\mathbb{G}
. Here, by a "semiclassical limit", we mean taking the "quantum deformation parameter(s)"
q\sim 1+\hbar=1+\frac{2\pi}{k}
to first order and applying the decategorification
\lambda
.

Theorem 4.23
.

Suppose
\lambda
satisfies hypothesis (H), then the semiclassical limit of
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
is dual to the Lie 2-bialgebra
(\mathfrak{G}=\operatorname{Lie}\mathbb{G};\delta)
.

Proof.

By
Lemma
4.22
,
\lambda
sends the categorical
R
-matrix on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
to a 2-
R
-matrix on
C_{q}(\mathbb{G})
. The statement then follows upon taking the limit
q\rightarrow 1
by the results of Appendix B in
[
44
]
.

∎

In other words, through hypothesis (H), the 2-graph operators described above does indeed give a quantization of 2-Chern-Simons theory on the lattice.

Remark 4.7
.

We emphasize here that the notion of a Hopf (co)category, possibly equipped with a cobraiding, that we have defined in §
4.3
is completely independent of Hypothesis (H).
Definition
4.13
serves to relate the quantum 2-holonomy operators described by the Hopf category to the higher-gauge fields described by the Hopf 2-algebra. If one is content with constructing TQFTs directly from the 2-holonomies/Hopf category, then one would not need to invoke Hypothesis (H) at all.

\Diamond

4.3.3
The 4d categorical ladder

The above set of results is a realization of the categorical ladder proposal
[
143
,
9
,
54
]
, which states that 4d TQFTs are determined by a Hopf monoidal category; see fig.
1
. In this context, the Hopf category
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
plays a role analogous to the Hopf algebra of Chern-Simons observables defined on the lattice in
[
1
]
.

Figure 1:
The categorical ladder as proposed in
[
143
,
9
,
54
]
, which gives a prescription for how the observables in a higher-dimensional TQFT should behave. Here, the vertical axis is the dimension and the horizontal axis denotes the operation of taking modules.

As such, in analogy with the seminal work of Witten
[
178
]
, observables of 2-Chern-Simons theory (modulo 2-gauge transformations) should then be described by an assignment of elements of the categorified quantum coordinate ring
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
to the so-called "2-ribbons"
[
13
,
40
,
101
]
— namely surfaces
\Sigma
bounding incoming and outgoing links/tangles — this will be made precise in the next paper in the series.

The key question now is to characterize the
quantum 2-gauge transformations
, denoted by
\tilde{{\cal C}}=\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
, on the 2-graph operators; we will show in §
6.1
that, under appropriate coherence conditions,
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
is a
monoidal
measureable module over
\tilde{{\cal C}}=\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
. The companion paper
[
48
]
then extracts a
canonical higher ribbon
structure out of the corresponding braided tensor 2-representation 2-category
\operatorname{2Rep}(\tilde{{\cal C}};\tilde{R})
.

The 2-tangle hypothesis
[
13
]
then implies that such an assignment completes — à la, for instance, a 4-dimensional version of handlebody surgery
[
79
,
41
,
30
]
— into a
2-Chern-Simons TQFT

Z_{2CS}^{\mathbb{G}}:\operatorname{Bord}^{O}_{\langle 4,3\rangle+\epsilon}\rightarrow\mathsf{Vect}.

(4.96)

This is a direct categorification of the Reshetikhin-Turaev construction
[
149
,
148
,
169
,
170
]
to 4-dimensions. The ultimate goal of this project is to construct the functor (
4.96
).

5
Categorical quantum 2-gauge transformations

Equipped with the knowledge that 2-graph operators form various interrelated Hopf structures, we are going to introduce a Hopf structure on 2-gauge transformations such that
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
consist of
covariant
elements under the 2-gauge transformation
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
-representation. Further, the Hopf structures on the two sides shall be compatible, in the sense that
\Lambda
defines a Hopf module structure.

The bounded linear operators
U
making the 2-gauge transformations
\Lambda
concrete strictly speaking now act on spaces of formal power series of sections over
X
. In this way, the groupoid of 2-gauge parameters
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
themselves acquire a dependence on the formal parameter
\hbar
, as hence are themselves operator-valued formal power series. However, as most of what we will prove in the following is algebraic, this will not play a major role, hence we shall keep the dependence on
\hbar
and
q
implicit.

Remark 5.1
.

In the following, we shall consider an
additive
extension of the (geometric/classical) 2-gauge transformations
\tilde{{\cal C}}
. This is defined as an additive
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
-graded Hopf category internal to
\mathsf{Meas}
; see
[
164
]
for a definition of a monoidal category graded by a 2-group. The homogeneous elements
\zeta\in\tilde{{\cal C}}
are determined by an element
\{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})\}_{(v,e)}\in\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
, hence we will often make arguements directly with
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
.
Lemma
5.3
later will treat this in more detail.

\Diamond

5.1
Coproducts on the 2-gauge transformations

Recall the
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
-module structure of the 2-graph operators is defined as a map
\Lambda
from the decorated 1-graphs
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
into automorphisms of the 2-graph operators
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
, which is realized concretely on each 2-graph operator as sheaves of bounded linear operators
\Gamma_{c}(H^{X})\rightarrow\Gamma_{c}((\Lambda H)^{X})
. We have previously noted that there are sheaf isomorphisms witnessing the compositions of 2-gauge transformations

\Lambda_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}\cdot\Lambda_{(a_{v}^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}_{e})}\cong\Lambda_{(a_{v}a_{v}^{\prime},\gamma_{e}(a_{v}\rhd\gamma_{e}^{\prime}))}

horizontally, and also vertically

\Lambda_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e_{1}})}\circ\Lambda_{(a_{v^{\prime}},\gamma_{e_{2}})}=\Lambda_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{e_{2}})},\qquad a_{v^{\prime}}=a_{v}t(\gamma_{e})

on adjacent 1-graphs
v\xrightarrow{e_{1}}v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e_{2}}v^{\prime\prime}
.

Now the point is that
\Lambda
should endow
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
with the structure of a Hopf module over the 2-gauge transformations
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
(or its additive completion
\tilde{{\cal C}}
). In order for this to be the case, a
coproduct

\tilde{\Delta}:\tilde{{\cal C}}\rightarrow\tilde{{\cal C}}\times\tilde{{\cal C}}

akin to §
4.1
, must be specified.

Definition 5.1
.

We say the action functor
\Lambda:\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
has the
categorical quantum derivation property
iff there exist sheaf identifications such that

\Lambda_{-}\circ(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\cong(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\circ(\Lambda\otimes\Lambda)_{\tilde{\Delta}}\,.

(5.1)

The reason why this condition is named such is given in
Remark
5.2
. We shall prove in
Proposition
6.4
later that (
5.1
) is indeed necessary for the monoidal module structure.

In the following, we shall instead focus on a more geometric interpretation of the coproduct
\tilde{\Delta}
on
\tilde{{\cal C}}
.

Remark 5.2
.

Let us return for the moment to the case of the ordinary compact semisimple Lie group
G
. Recall the coproduct on
U\mathfrak{g}
is primitive
\tilde{\Delta}(X)=X\otimes 1+1\otimes X
. The condition analogous to (
5.1
) in the decategorified classical case then reproduces the
Leibniz rule

\Lambda_{X}(fg)=(\Lambda_{X}f)g+f(\Lambda_{X}g),\qquad X\in U\mathfrak{g},\qquad f,g\in C(G),

which identifies
\Lambda:U\mathfrak{g}\otimes C(G)\rightarrow C(G)
as the canonical action of
U\mathfrak{g}
by derivations on the functions
C(G)

[
159
]
. This explains why (
5.1
) was called the "quantum derivation" property: the action of
\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G})
behaves like a "derivation", and give further support for the interpretation that
\mathbb{U}_{q}\mathfrak{G}
is the categorical version of the quantum enveloping algebra. See also §
6.1.1
later.

\Diamond

5.2
Geometry of the coproduct
\tilde{\Delta}

As we have mentioned, the condition (
5.1
) is necessary for the Hopf category
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
to be a Hopf module (as a measureable category) under 2-gauge transformations
\Lambda:\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
. Geometrically, this condition also has an interpretation in terms of the composition of faces and edges in the graph complex
\Gamma
, similar to what was described in §
4.1
.

Let us for now focus on the classical case to make the geometry more explicit. If we write, in Sweedler notation,

\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}=\sum\zeta_{(1)}\times\zeta_{(2)},\qquad\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}=\sum\Lambda_{\zeta_{(1)}}\times\Lambda_{\zeta_{(2)}},

then the point of (
5.1
) (as well as the introduction of
\tilde{\Delta}
) is to ensure that the geometric picture in fig.
2
is consistent.

Figure 2:
A coproduct
\tilde{\Delta}
on the 2-gauge transformation parameters
\zeta\in\tilde{{\cal C}}
manifests naturally from the composition of the 2-graphs.

Let us make this more explicit in the following.

5.2.1
Graph and 2-gauge transformations

Consider a face
(e,f)\in\Gamma^{2}
which is obtained from the horizontal composition of two half-faces
(e_{1},f_{1}),(e_{2},f_{2})
. The 2-graph operators
\phi_{(e_{1},f_{1})},\phi^{\prime}_{(e_{2},f_{2})}\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})
localized on such a face must then agree on the attaching edge
(e_{1},f_{1})\cap(e_{2},f_{2})
.

We wish to examine the 2-gauge transformation properties of such a configuration of classical, geometric 2-graph operators. For this, it would be useful in the following to introduce the following notation which detects the proximity of a 2-gauge transformation from the decorated 2-graph localized at the face
(e,f)
.

For each face
(e,f)\in\Gamma^{2}
with root edge
e
, we define a 2-gauge action
\Lambda^{(e,f)}
as follows,

\Lambda^{(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})}_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}=\begin{cases}\Lambda_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e^{\prime}})}&;e^{\prime}=e\\
\Lambda_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e^{\prime}})}^{-1}&;e^{\prime}=e\ast\partial f\\
\Lambda_{(a_{v},1)}&;v=t(e^{\prime})=s(e)\\
\Lambda_{(a_{v},{\bf 1}_{a_{v}})}^{-1}&;v=s(e^{\prime})=t(e)\\
\Lambda_{(1_{v},({\bf 1}_{1_{v}})_{e})}&;\text{otherwise}\end{cases},\qquad(a_{v},\gamma_{e})\in\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}

where
\bar{e}
denotes the orientation reversal of the edge
e
, whose source
s(\bar{e})
is the target
t(e)
of
e
. This notation. Thus, if
(e,f)
is composite, then we are able to deduce its action by 2-gauge transformations locally by looking at how its 1-graph boundary
\Gamma^{1}
is composite.

Denote by

\phi\mapsto\Lambda_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e_{1}})}^{(e,f)}\phi

the
localized
2-gauge transformation action on a 2-graph operator at
(e,f).
Given the composite
(v,e)=(v_{1},e_{1})\cup(v_{2},e_{2})
of the boundary edge, we have

\Lambda_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e_{1}})}^{(e_{1},f_{1})}\phi,\qquad\Lambda^{(e_{2},f_{2})}_{(a_{v_{2}},\gamma_{e_{2}})}\phi,

where
v_{1,2}
denotes the source vertex of the edge
e_{1,2}
. Recall that the left-action
\Lambda
is defined by precomposing with a horizontal conjugation action (
3.1
), hence we see at the level of the decorated 2-graphs that

\operatorname{hAd}^{-1}_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e_{1}})}(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})\cdot_{h}\operatorname{hAd}^{-1}_{(a_{v_{2}},\gamma_{e_{2}})}(h_{e_{2}},b_{f_{2}})=\operatorname{hAd}^{-1}_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{e_{2}})}(h_{e},b_{f}),

where we have noted that
v_{2}
must also be the target vertex of
e_{1}
from the geometry, and by definition
(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})\cdot_{h}(h_{e_{1}},b_{f_{1}})=(h_{e},b_{f})
. This dictates how the 2-gauge transformations act on tensor products of 2-graph operators: evaluating this identity yields the
covariance
of the graph-cutting coproduct:

\displaystyle\Lambda_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e})}^{(e,f)}\phi

\displaystyle\cong\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{2}=s(e_{2})\\
\gamma_{e}=\gamma_{e_{1}}\gamma_{e_{2}}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{(a_{v_{1}},\gamma_{e_{1}})}^{(e_{1},f_{1})}(\phi_{(1)})\otimes\Lambda^{(e_{2},f_{2})}_{(a_{v_{2}},\gamma_{e_{2}})}(\phi_{(2)})\right),

from which we can deduce the coproduct

\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta_{(v,e)}}=\bigoplus_{\zeta_{(v_{1},e_{1})}\circ\zeta_{(v_{2},e_{2})}=\zeta_{(v,e)}}\zeta_{(e_{1},f_{1})}\times\zeta_{(e_{2},f_{2})}

(5.2)

for 2-gauge parameters localized on composite 1-graphs.

In this classical undeformed setting, the
R
-matrix is trivial, as such the expressions (
5.2
) give precisely the classical version of (
5.1
). The Sweedler notation for the coproduct is also "grouplike". We shall see in the following section that, in order to have a quantum version of (
5.1
), we must have a non-trivial
R
-matrix on the 2-gauge parameters
\tilde{{\cal C}}
as well.

5.2.2
The induced
R
-matrix on
\tilde{{\cal C}}

To promote the compatibility conditions (
5.2
) to the quantum theory, we are going to assume that there exist elements
\tilde{R}\in\tilde{{\cal C}}\times\tilde{{\cal C}}
such that there are sheaf identifications

\displaystyle R\otimes\big(\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})\big)\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{R}\cdot\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})

\displaystyle\big(\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})\big)\otimes R\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}\cdot\tilde{R}}(\phi\times\phi^{\prime})

(5.3)

for any
\phi\times\phi^{\prime}\in(\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma}))^{\times 2}
and homogeneous elements
\zeta\in\tilde{{\cal C}}
determined by
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
.
9
9

9

The ”
\cdot
” on the right-hand sides denote the product of 2-gauge transformations in
\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
.

The purpose of this condition is that, if
\tilde{R}
induces a cobraiding for the quantum 2-gauge parameters
\tilde{{\cal C}}
— that is, there are natural transformations

\tilde{\mathsf{T}}_{(a_{v},\gamma_{e})}:(\tilde{\Delta}^{\text{op}})(a_{v},\gamma_{e})\cdot\tilde{R}\cong\tilde{R}\cdot\tilde{\Delta}_{h}(a_{v},\gamma_{e}),

(5.4)

for each
\zeta=(a_{v},\gamma_{e})\in\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma^{1}}
, then (
5.1
) follows. We will now prove this.

Theorem 5.2
.

Suppose there exists
\tilde{R}\in\tilde{{\cal C}}\times\tilde{{\cal C}}
and the natural transformation (
5.4
) which identifies a cobraiding on
\tilde{{\cal C}}
. If the identifications (
5.3
) exist, then the 2-gauge transformation functor
\Lambda
has the categorical quantum derivation property as in
Definition
5.1
.

Proof.

We are going to leverage the expression (
4.13
) for the quantum tensor product
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}
. If
(e,f),(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are delocalized faces (namely their intersection is empty), then from (
4.3
) the Poisson bracket is trivial, hence the product reduces to the classical one
\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}=\otimes
. Thus suppose from now on that
(e,f),(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
are not delocalized.

Let
\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}
be a localized 2-graph operator for which
(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})=(e,f)\cup(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
is composite face. Then beginning from the right-hand side of (
5.1
), we have

\displaystyle(-\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}-)\big(\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}(\phi_{(e,f)}\otimes\phi_{(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})})\big)

\displaystyle=(-\otimes-)[R,\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}]_{c}

\displaystyle\cong(-\otimes-)\big(R\otimes\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}-(\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})})^{\text{op}}\otimes\tilde{R}^{T}\big),

where the superscript "op" means that the two tensor factors are swapped (note
\Delta^{\text{op}}
is the same as
\sigma\circ\Delta
). Consider the first term: using the condition (
5.3
), then (
5.4
) and (
5.3
) again gives us

\displaystyle R\otimes\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{R}\cdot\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}^{\text{op}}\cdot\tilde{R}}\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}

\displaystyle\cong\big(\Delta^{\text{op}}_{\mathrm{h}}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}\Lambda^{T}_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}^{\text{op}}\cdot\tilde{R}}\big)^{\text{op}}=\big((\Delta^{\text{op}}_{\mathrm{h}}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}\otimes\tilde{R}^{T})\Lambda^{T}_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}^{\text{op}}}\big)^{\text{op}}

\displaystyle\cong\Lambda_{\tilde{\Delta}_{\zeta}}\big((\Delta_{h}^{\text{op}}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}\otimes R^{T})\big)^{\text{op}},

where "
\otimes
" here denotes the monoidal structure on
\mathfrak{C}_{q}(\mathbb{G}^{\Gamma})^{\times 2}
.

If we perform a contraction
(-\otimes-)
, then we can make use of (
5.2
) to get

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\zeta}\big((-\otimes-)(\Delta^{\text{op}}_{\mathrm{h}}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}\otimes R^{T})^{\text{op}}\cong\Lambda_{\zeta}(-\otimes-)(R\otimes\Delta_{h}\phi_{(e^{\prime\prime},f^{\prime\prime})}),

which is nothing but the first term of
\Lambda_{\zeta}(\phi_{(e,f)}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\phi_{(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})})
. The same argument takes care of the second term, whence we finally achieve (
5.1
)
